Visual witness description of PDF page 31 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A diagram with two horizontal rows of circular nodes representing paths: top row labeled "left copy" containing nodes $i_0$, $i_1$, $i_2$, $i_3$, with $i_0$ crossed out with a diagonal slash and labeled "delete" above; bottom row labeled "right copy" containing nodes $i_0$, $i_1$, $i_2$, $i_3$, with $i_3$ crossed out with a diagonal slash and labeled "delete" below. Three upward-diagonal arrows connect nodes from the right copy to the left copy: from right $i_0$ to left $i_1$ labeled $y_1$, from right $i_1$ to left $i_2$ labeled $y_2$, and from right $i_2$ to left $i_3$ labeled $y_3$.
```


We calculate a lift density before estimating it. Treat the left as the observed copy, fix $\mathcal D$, the tags and list, and condition on the paths $\eta$ outside $S=\{i_1,\ldots,i_m\}$. Put $B_S=\prod_{l\in S}B_l$. Let $c_S(o;\eta)$ be the product of all individual cutoff indicators on $S$ and all checks involving at least one label in $S$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-path-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-path-law">9</a>, the original conditional law is <a id="eq:local-reference"></a>$$\label{eq:local-reference}
 \lambda_S(\,\mathrm do\mid\eta)
       =\frac{c_S(o;\eta)}{Z_S(\eta)}B_S(\,\mathrm do),
 \qquad Z_S(\eta)=\int c_S(o;\eta)B_S(\,\mathrm do)>0.$$ Checks wholly outside $S$ are fixed and do not enter this formula.

<a id="eq:local-proposal"></a>
The generation kernel on the changed left paths has a density $k_S(o,\eta,z)$ with respect to $B_S(\,\mathrm dz)$. To verify this precisely, write $B_l^y$ for the free path law of label $l$ conditional on midpoint $y$, and $g_l$ for its Gaussian midpoint density. A pair with observed label $l$ and auxiliary label $l'$ has proposal measure $$\label{eq:local-proposal}
 \frac{\mathbf 1_C(z_l(0))}{g_l(z_l(0))}
                  B_l(\,\mathrm dz_l)B_{l'}^{z_l(0)}(\,\mathrm dz'_{l'}).$$ Multiplication by its success indicators and division by its success probability describes the actual insertion kernel. Sequential integration of the auxiliary paths gives $k_S$. This is a density for the observed paths only: the paired paths have a common midpoint, and their joint law has no density against two independent bridges.

<a id="stmt-lem-local-likelihood"></a>
<div id="lem:local-likelihood" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:local-likelihood"></a>
<a id="eq:local-centering"></a>
<a id="eq:local-likelihood-bound"></a>
**Lemma 15** (The centered conditional likelihood). *Include the second tag indicator but omit its divisor $T_{w'}$. On admissible output paths the left likelihood is $$\label{eq:local-likelihood}
 L_S(z;\eta)=\int c_S(o;\eta)h_{w'i_m}(o)
                      k_S(o,\eta,z)B_S(\,\mathrm do).$$ It obeys $$\label{eq:local-centering}
 F_S=L_S-h_{w'i_m},\qquad
                 \int F_S\,\,\mathrm d\lambda_S(\cdot\mid\eta)=0,$$ and $$\label{eq:local-likelihood-bound}
                     0\le L_S\le C_xD^{C\sqrt x}.$$ These assertions remain valid after deleting specified outside checks consistently from the reference, the original-path integral, and all insertion tests and their normalizing probabilities.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-local-likelihood"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-local-likelihood</summary>

*Proof.* Before taking the likelihood, the generated density against $B_S$ is the right side of <a href="#eq:local-likelihood" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-likelihood">[eq:local-likelihood]</a> divided by $Z_S$. The output kernel is supported on $c_S(z;\eta)=1$, where the reference density against $B_S$ is $1/Z_S$. Thus the normalizer cancels. Since each generation kernel has mass one, its support also gives $$\int c_S(z;\eta)L_S(z;\eta)B_S(\,\mathrm dz)
       =\int c_S(o;\eta)h_{w'i_m}(o)B_S(\,\mathrm do).$$ Division by $Z_S$ proves <a href="#eq:local-centering" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-centering">[eq:local-centering]</a>. The subtracted indicator there is evaluated on the reference output, whereas the indicator inside <a href="#eq:local-likelihood" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-likelihood">[eq:local-likelihood]</a> is evaluated on the integrated original path.

On $C_k$, the factor $g_{i_k}^{-1}$ in <a href="#eq:local-proposal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-proposal">[eq:local-proposal]</a> is at most $CD^{Cx}$, since its distance from the mean is absolutely bounded and $t^{-1}=x\log D$. Every success denominator costs at most two. Dropping unused checks, and integrating the auxiliary conditional bridges, bounds the observed kernel density by $(CD^{Cx})^m2^m$. The original-path integral has mass at most one. Since $m=O(x^{-1/2})$, this proves <a href="#eq:local-likelihood-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-likelihood-bound">[eq:local-likelihood-bound]</a>.

Deleting checks preserves output support in the corresponding admissible set and only improves the insertion lower bounds. The same argument applies on that modified conditional fiber. For later integrals we also define these kernels at outside values satisfying all individual cutoffs, even if a check wholly outside $S$ fails: that outside-only check is ignored. The insertion bounds hold at these histories, and the formulas represent actual conditional likelihoods wherever the outside reference constraints hold. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Cancellation in a fourth moment

The likelihood for one chosen list need not be close to one. Averaging many possible tags and interior labels produces the needed small error. Conditional centering cancels terms involving an isolated list; the terms that survive have either repeated labels or at least two rare interactions between lists. We make both statements precise.

<a id="eq:local-centered-fourth"></a>
For fixed tags and data, put a bar over a quantity to denote averaging over the route and interior label choices. We claim that, for sufficiently small fixed $x$, $$\label{eq:local-centered-fourth}
 \mathbb E\left[\mathbf 1_{\mathcal G_{v,w'}}
       \sum_{i\in I}\frac{h_{vi}}{T_v}
       \left|\sum_{i_m\ne i}\frac{\overline{F_S}}{T_{w'}}\right|^4
       \right]\le C_{x,j}D^{-1-1/20}.$$ Each tag sum is restricted to the fixed enlargement of its cell and to labels in $I$. We first prove the conditional interaction estimate needed to expand this fourth moment.

Fix four lists with the same first tag $i$, and let $S_a$ be their changed sets, $1\le a\le4$. Condition on $\mathcal D$ and on all paths outside $U=\bigcup_aS_a$. Join two lists if their changed sets intersect; the connected components are called blocks. A singleton block contains one list, not necessarily one label. Distinct blocks have disjoint label sets. Introduce a switch $\sigma_e\in\{0,1\}$ for each unordered pair $e$ of blocks. Turning it off deletes all checks between those blocks from the following three places:

1.  the union reference on $U$;

2.  the original-path indicators in each likelihood integral;

3.  the insertion indicators and their success probabilities.

In the last two places, paths outside a given changed set are the reference paths. All individual cutoffs are retained. The unchanged left path of the common tag $i$ is outside every block, so checks against it are never switched. A newly generated right path with label $i$, however, belongs to its generating block; its checks against other blocks are switched. It is never checked against the left copy of the same label. These rules also specify the switches when two lists overlap.

Let $c_U^\sigma$ denote the switched union indicator and $F_a^\sigma$ the switched centered likelihood. Define the unnormalized integral <a id="eq:local-switch-integral"></a>$$\label{eq:local-switch-integral}
 G(\sigma)=\int c_U^\sigma(z)
                   \prod_{a=1}^4F_a^\sigma(z)B_U(\,\mathrm dz).$$ The conditional fourth-moment summand before switching is $G(\boldsymbol1)/Z_U$, where $Z_U=\int c_U^{\boldsymbol1}\,\mathrm dB_U$. We hold this denominator fixed throughout the switch expansion. Successive single-path insertions give $Z_U^{-1}\le C_x$, because $U$ has at most $4C_x$ labels.

For a block-pair edge $e$, let $\mathcal Q_e$ be the finite list of distinct-label pairs whose checks are controlled by that switch. It includes checks using generated auxiliary paths and original paths, with bounded multiplicity. All its labels and endpoints are fixed by the data and the four lists.

<a id="stmt-lem-local-switches"></a>
<div id="lem:local-switches" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:local-one-switch"></a>
<a id="eq:local-two-switches"></a>
**Lemma 16** (One and two switched interactions). *Suppose the four changed sets are either pairwise disjoint, or have exactly one independent label identification. Write $\Delta_e$ for the finite difference from $\sigma_e=0$ to $\sigma_e=1$. Uniformly in all other switch settings, $$\label{eq:local-one-switch}
 \frac{|\Delta_eG|}{Z_U}
       \le C_xD^{C\sqrt x}\sum_{(l,k)\in\mathcal Q_e}\beta_{lk}.$$ If the changed sets are pairwise disjoint and $e\ne f$, then $$\label{eq:local-two-switches}
 \frac{|\Delta_e\Delta_fG|}{Z_U}
   \le C_xD^{C\sqrt x}
       \sum_{\substack{(l,k)\in\mathcal Q_e\\
                       (l',k')\in\mathcal Q_f}}
                                 \beta_{lk}\beta_{l'k'}.$$ The sums have at most $C_x$ terms. Moreover $\beta_{lk}\le C_xD^{-19/100}$ for sufficiently large $D$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-local-switches"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-local-switches</summary>

*Proof.* Expand each centered factor as $L_S-h_{w'i_m}$, then use <a href="#eq:local-likelihood" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-likelihood">[eq:local-likelihood]</a> for each likelihood. Each such term has reference output bridges on $U$, a separate collection of latent original bridges for each likelihood, and its generated auxiliary bridges. The original integrations remain separate even when their output coordinates coincide. All individual cutoff indicators remain in place when estimating collisions.

First consider singleton blocks. Combine the observed bridge factor in <a href="#eq:local-proposal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-proposal">[eq:local-proposal]</a> with its midpoint density and auxiliary conditional bridge. The result is exactly the common-uniform-midpoint pair proposal. After unused checks and bounded success denominators are discarded, the variables within a singleton block therefore have a product of ordinary original bridges and these pair proposals. If its centered factor contributes the subtracted indicator, it uses ordinary output bridges instead. These base measures are independent between blocks: their cells and endpoints depend only on $\mathcal D$. The common midpoint inside one pair is preserved throughout.

A difference of a check indicator forces failure of one of the checks controlled by that edge. Failure of <a href="#eq:soft-check" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:soft-check">[eq:soft-check]</a> requires a collision within the interaction range $r$. A union bound reduces it to one of the label pairs in $\mathcal Q_e$. Given an allowed opponent path, integrating a plain bridge or a marginal of the uniform-midpoint pair proposal, with its individual cutoffs retained, costs at most $C_x\beta_{lk}$ by <a href="#eq:beta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:beta">[eq:beta]</a> and the uniform-midpoint form of <a href="#eq:midpoint-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:midpoint-cost">[eq:midpoint-cost]</a>.

Success denominators require the same conclusion. At fixed history a success probability can be written using a fresh proposal $Q$ as $$p(\sigma)=\int Q(\,\mathrm dz)\,c(z)
                         \prod_e A_e(z)^{\sigma_e},
                  \qquad p(\sigma)\ge\tfrac12.$$ Here $c$ contains the individual cutoffs and unswitched checks, while $A_e$ is the product of the checks controlled by $e$ at this stage. We use $A^0=1$ also when $A=0$. A first difference introduces $A_e-1$; a mixed difference introduces $(A_e-1)(A_f-1)$. They force the indicated one or two collisions. The identity $$p^{-1}-q^{-1}=\frac{q-p}{pq}$$ and its difference in a second variable bound the second reciprocal difference by a constant times $$|\Delta_e\Delta_fp|
       +\max_\sigma|\Delta_ep(\sigma)|
                       \max_\sigma|\Delta_fp(\sigma)|.$$ The product of first differences is represented with independent fresh proposals. Every such proposal belongs to the block whose denominator produced it. The lower bound for $p$ holds at all histories under consideration, since only individual cutoffs are required of earlier paths when unused checks are ignored.

The finite-product difference rule now bounds a first difference by a finite sum of integrals with one forced cross-block collision, and a second difference by ones with two specified cross-block collisions. Unused denominators cost only $C_x$. With no label identification, either end of one edge can be integrated as a singleton. For two distinct edges, their graph consists of a two-edge tree or two disjoint edges. In the tree case fix all variables at the central vertex and integrate its two leaves in succession. Each leaf participates in one forced collision, so these integrations give the two $\beta$ factors. No independence between the two collision events at the central block is required. For disjoint edges the two integrations are separate. Fresh proposals from denominator differences obey the same argument after assigning them to their generating blocks.

With one independent identification the block sizes are $2,1,1$. Every edge has a singleton end, which supplies the collision factor in <a href="#eq:local-one-switch" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-one-switch">[eq:local-one-switch]</a>. After integrating that singleton, the remaining repeated output coordinates in the block of size two are bounded by the observed density estimate <a href="#eq:local-likelihood-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-likelihood-bound">[eq:local-likelihood-bound]</a>: bound any surplus factors $g_l^{-1}\mathbf 1_C$ by $CD^{Cx}$ and integrate auxiliary conditional bridges. Across the four lists this costs at most $C_xD^{C\sqrt x}$. The same harmless bound covers all remaining factors in the disjoint case. This proves both difference estimates. The argument allows arbitrary settings of unused switches; additional differences are bounded by finite sums of these estimates at those settings. Finally <a href="#eq:beta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:beta">[eq:beta]</a> gives $\beta_{lk}\le b_D(1+2/s_1)\le C_xD^{-19/100}$. ◻

</details>

We can now prove <a href="#eq:local-centered-fourth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-centered-fourth">[eq:local-centered-fourth]</a>. Expanding the fourth power gives four independently chosen lists, conditional on the common first tag and the data. There are at most $C_xDf_0$ candidates for each tag or interior slot, by the good endpoint counts and the tube restriction. A normalized tag counting weight and every interior-choice probability are at most $C_{x,j}/(Df_0)$. Split the sums by the tag mean-sites and the route site patterns; there are only $C_x$ patterns. We retain the original weights in each subsum, without conditioning on a pattern and renormalizing.

<a id="eq:local-many-identifications"></a>
An equality pattern with $q$ independent identifications has $q$ fewer free label choices. Dominating all slot weights by their point bounds and counting the remaining choices gives total weight at most $C_{x,j}(Df_0)^{-q}$. A triple equality counts as two identifications. These are joint counting estimates; they assert no point-mass bound conditional on the occurrence of a coincidence. There are only $C_x$ equality patterns. Consequently all patterns with $q\ge2$ contribute, by <a href="#eq:local-likelihood-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-likelihood-bound">[eq:local-likelihood-bound]</a>, at most $$\label{eq:local-many-identifications}
                         C_{x,j}D^{-2+2x+C\sqrt x}.$$

For $q=0$ or $1$, use the switches above. For an edge set $E$, let $\Delta_E=\prod_{e\in E}\Delta_e$. The elementary Boolean identity is <a id="eq:local-boolean"></a>$$\label{eq:local-boolean}
                   G(\boldsymbol1)=\sum_E\Delta_EG(\boldsymbol0).$$ If the graph with edge set $E$ has an isolated singleton block, its term vanishes. Indeed all edges from that block are off in every evaluation comprising the difference. Its variables separate from the others, and their integral is $$\int c_S F_S\,\,\mathrm dB_S=0$$ by <a href="#eq:local-centering" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-centering">[eq:local-centering]</a>. This is why the common denominator $Z_U$ was held fixed rather than switched.

When $q=1$ there are three blocks, two of them singletons, so a nonzero term must contain an edge. Its identification cost and <a href="#eq:local-one-switch" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-one-switch">[eq:local-one-switch]</a> give total contribution at most <a id="eq:local-one-identification"></a>$$\label{eq:local-one-identification}
                    C_{x,j}D^{-1+x-19/100+C\sqrt x}.$$ When $q=0$, all four blocks are singletons and a nonzero term has at least two distinct edges. Apply <a href="#eq:local-two-switches" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-two-switches">[eq:local-two-switches]</a> to any two of them, and bound any remaining differences by a finite sum of evaluations with the other switches fixed.

<a id="eq:label-average"></a>
<a id="eq:local-no-identification"></a>
One of these two collision factors can be averaged over a label slot. Every cross-block check has at least one label in a changed set, whereas the common tag $i$ belongs to none of those sets. The endpoint bound <a href="#eq:flag-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flag-count">[eq:flag-count]</a>, summed over dyadic annuli in a fixed neighborhood, gives uniformly in the other endpoint $y$ $$\label{eq:label-average}
 \frac{C_{x,j}}{Df_0}
       \sum_{k\text{ in a slot range}}
                 \frac1{s_1\vee d_K(y,Y_k)}
 \le C_{x,j}\left(1+\frac{D^{1/100}}{Df_0s_1}\right)
 \le C_{x,j}.$$ For example, a dyadic annulus of radius $h\le1$ contributes at most $C_{x,j}(h^2+D^{1/100}/(Df_0h))$; the innermost ball is bounded at radius $s_1$, and the unit-scale and larger annuli use the unit-count bound. The geometric sums give the displayed expression. The same estimate holds with $Z_k$ in place of $Y_k$. Thus averaging this factor costs at most $C_{x,j}b_D$. Bound the other factor by $C_xD^{-19/100}$. In these positive bounds we may remove all further noncoincidence restrictions and enlarge the label sums. The total contribution with no identification is therefore at most $$\label{eq:local-no-identification}
                    C_{x,j}D^{-19/100+C\sqrt x}b_D.$$ The first-tag factor $h_{vi}$ is fixed in every conditional integral, so it does not disturb centering. Its total normalized tag sum on good data is bounded by $C_{x,j}$. Equations <a href="#eq:local-many-identifications" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-many-identifications">[eq:local-many-identifications]</a>–<a href="#eq:local-no-identification" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-no-identification">[eq:local-no-identification]</a> prove <a href="#eq:local-centered-fourth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-centered-fourth">[eq:local-centered-fourth]</a> once $x$ is chosen sufficiently small. Indeed $b_D=C_xD^{-1}(\log D)^C$, and all three powers then save more than $1/20$ beyond $D^{-1}$.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### The local comparison after projection

<a id="eq:local-lift-error"></a>
Let $\ell_v$ and $\ell_{w'}$ be the two lifted densities of $\nu_{v,w'}$ relative to their augmented references. On retained data with first tag in $I$, the left density is $$\ell_v=\sum_{i_m\ne i}\frac{\overline{L_S}}{T_{w'}};$$ set it to zero on the omitted part of the reference. The centered subtraction sums to $A_{w'}-M_{w'}'$. In fact the candidate mean range contains every tag with nonzero $h_{w'i_m}$, by confinement, and the excluded first tag has $h_{w'i}=0$ because the cells are disjoint. Thus on the retained part $$\label{eq:local-lift-error}
 \ell_v-1=\sum_{i_m\ne i}\frac{\overline{F_S}}{T_{w'}}
                              +(A_{w'}-1)-M_{w'}'.$$

<a id="stmt-prop-local-palm"></a>
<div id="prop:local-palm" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:local-lift-fourth"></a>
**Proposition 17** (Adjacent deletion measures). *For sufficiently small fixed $x>0$, depending on $u$, the common measure constructed above satisfies $$\label{eq:local-lift-fourth}
 \mathbb E_{v\sim w'}\left[
       \int|\ell_v-1|^4\,\,\mathrm dP_{\mathrm{aug},v}
       +\int|\ell_{w'}-1|^4\,\,\mathrm dP_{\mathrm{aug},w'}\right]
                         \le C_jD^{-1-u/8}.$$ Consequently the local comparison <a href="#eq:local-fourth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-fourth">[eq:local-fourth]</a> holds. That bound, with a fixed change of constant, also holds when only adjacent pairs inside the same $R$-cell are retained in the average.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-local-palm"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-local-palm</summary>

*Proof.* Apply $|a+b+c|^4\le27(|a|^4+|b|^4+|c|^4)$ to <a href="#eq:local-lift-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-lift-error">[eq:local-lift-error]</a>. The centered term is controlled by <a href="#eq:local-centered-fourth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-centered-fourth">[eq:local-centered-fourth]</a>. Off the negligible count tail, the count-fluctuation term is at most $$\mathbb E[A_v|A_{w'}-1|^4]
     \le C_jD^x\mathbb E|A_{w'}-1|^4+O(D^{-A})$$ for any prescribed fixed $A$. Averaging adjacent cells and using <a href="#eq:flag-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flag-moments">[eq:flag-moments]</a> bounds it by $C_j(D^{-1-u/6+x}+D^{-2+3x})$. Likewise, $$\mathbb E[A_v(M_{w'}')^4]
       \le C_jD^{4x}\mathbb EM_{w'}'+O(D^{-A})
       \le C_{x,j}D^{-1-1/8+4x}+O(D^{-A}).$$ The part where $\ell_v$ was set to zero contributes its reference mass, controlled by <a href="#eq:tag-omission" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tag-omission">[eq:tag-omission]</a> and <a href="#eq:good-omission" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:good-omission">[eq:good-omission]</a>. Taking $x<u/24$ in addition to the earlier smallness conditions makes all these terms bounded by $C_jD^{-1-u/8}$.

For the right lift, regard the right copy as observed, reverse the list indices, and keep the actual generation order. Its unchanged tag is now $i_m$ and its changed set excludes $i_m$. The density formula, switched centering, and collision integrations are unchanged: they allow any fixed insertion order. This proves <a href="#eq:local-lift-fourth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-lift-fourth">[eq:local-lift-fourth]</a> for the two lifts of the same measure.

Under a measurable projection, the projected likelihood is the conditional expectation of the lifted likelihood. Conditional Jensen therefore transfers each fourth-moment bound to the density of $\nu_{v,w'}$ relative to $P_v$ or $P_{w'}$. Write $q(Y')$ for its Lebesgue density and put $y=s_v(Y')$, $z=s_{w'}(Y')$. Absolute continuity with respect to both references makes $q=0$ where either reference density vanishes. Inserting $\sqrt q$ between $y$ and $z$, and using $|1-\sqrt a|\le|1-a|$ for $a\ge0$, gives $$\frac{|y-z|^4}{y^2+z^2}
   \le 8\left[y^2\left|1-\frac q{y^2}\right|^4
               +z^2\left|1-\frac q{z^2}\right|^4\right].$$ Terms with zero prefactor are omitted. Integrating this inequality and applying the two projected bounds proves <a href="#eq:local-fourth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-fourth">[eq:local-fourth]</a>. Restricting the nonnegative edge sum to edges within $R$-cells only decreases it; the normalizations differ by a bounded factor. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:long-palm"></a>
### Distant deletion comparisons

We now compare the deletion measures for distant cells of side $R$. We prove <a href="#eq:distant-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distant-comparison">[eq:distant-comparison]</a> for the ordered $R$-cell pairs $\mathcal P_R$ defined at the beginning of Section <a href="#sec:paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:paths">4</a>. The local construction of Section <a href="#sec:local-palm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:local-palm">5</a> cannot simply be iterated: its route would have length growing with $K$, whereas even a fixed loss at each step would destroy the estimate. We instead retain the original paths near both ends of a long random route. In a product of two likelihoods, the resulting errors occur only when the changed portions of the two routes meet. A geometric input makes these meetings rare and controls their number exponentially.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### The geometric input and its application

We use the following complete statement of (OpenAI 2026b, Proposition 7.1) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[D](#ref-D)]. It concerns a random set of lattice sites only. The proof in (OpenAI 2026b, sec. 7) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[D](#ref-D)] uses folded paths and the unpredictable-path method of (Benjamini, Pemantle, and Peres 1998) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[BPP](#ref-BPP)].

<a id="stmt-prop-route-input"></a>
<div id="prop:route-input" class="proposition">

**Proposition 18** (Routes with rare encounters away from the common end). *On $\mathcal L_K=(\mathbb Z/K\mathbb Z)^3$, let $d_\infty$ be the periodic maximum distance. A random datum $\mathcal D$ determines a bad set $\mathcal B\subset\mathcal L_K$ satisfying $$\mathbb P(A\subset\mathcal B)\le p^{|A|}\qquad(A\subset\mathcal L_K).$$ Fix an integer contact radius $r_c\ge1$, an integer $q\ge0$, and $c_Q>0$. Let $Q$ be a projected lattice cube of side at most $c_QR$, and let $z$ be a site such that every $y\in Q$ has forward first-coordinate displacement from $z$ in $[.29K,.46K]$. There are constants $p_0,\kappa,c,C,C_0>0$ and $R_0$, depending only on the fixed parameters, with the following properties when $R\ge R_0$, $K\ge C_0R$, and $p\le p_0$.*

*Put $H=\lfloor R^{1/2}\rfloor$. There is an event $\mathcal E$, determined by $\mathcal D,z,Q$, on which one can sample simple good nearest-neighbor routes $\pi_y$ from $z$ to every $y\in Q$. Conditional on $\mathcal D$, different samples use independent fresh randomness. The event $\mathcal E$ includes the requirement that the $10R$-neighborhoods of $z$ and $Q$ contain no bad sites.*

<a id="eq:route-exponential"></a>
<a id="eq:route-rare"></a>
<a id="eq:route-rejection"></a>
*For $\pi=(v_0,\ldots,v_m)$ starting at $z$, put $$a(\pi)=\max\{i:d_\infty(v_i,z)\le H\},\qquad
 \pi^{\rm ch}=\{v_i:i>a(\pi)\}.$$ The initial portion $(v_0,\ldots,v_{a(\pi)})$ has at most $CH$ vertices. The analogous portion read backwards from the terminal site also has at most $CH$ vertices, and these two portions are disjoint. Let $\pi^{\rm ch,q}$ contain the vertices whose path index is within $q$ of an index in $\pi^{\rm ch}$. For independent samples $\pi_y,\pi_{y'}$, set $$J_c=\sum_{v\in\pi_y}\sum_{v'\in\pi_{y'}}
       \mathbf 1_{\{d_\infty(v,v')\le r_c\}},\qquad
 J_*=\sum_{v\in\pi_y^{\rm ch,q}}
       \sum_{v'\in\pi_{y'}^{\rm ch,q}}
       \mathbf 1_{\{d_\infty(v,v')\le r_c\}}.$$ Uniformly in the specified endpoints, $$\label{eq:route-exponential}
 \mathbb E_{\mathcal D}\bigl[\mathbf 1_{\mathcal E}
       \mathbb E_{\rm routes}(e^{\kappa J_c}\mid\mathcal D)\bigr]\le C.$$ For every deterministic probability mass function $\omega$ on $Q\times Q$ with $\omega(y,y')\le C_\omega R^{-6}$, $$\label{eq:route-rare}
 \sum_{y,y'\in Q}\omega(y,y')
   \mathbb E_{\mathcal D}\bigl[\mathbf 1_{\mathcal E}
       \mathbb P_{\rm routes}(J_*>0\mid\mathcal D)\bigr]
 \le CC_\omega R^{-c_g},\qquad
 c_g=\frac12\left(\frac{\log2}{\log3}-\frac12\right)>0.$$ Both estimates hold with a common terminal endpoint, reading the same route construction in reverse order. Finally, if $\mathcal M$ is the stated bad-free margin event, then $$\label{eq:route-rejection}
 \mathbb P(\mathcal M\setminus\mathcal E)
 \le K^3(Ap)^{\lfloor K/(10(r_c+2))\rfloor}+CR^3e^{-cR},$$ where $A$ is fixed and $Ap_0<1$.*

</div>

We check the hypotheses and fix how this result is used. The bad sites are those of Section <a href="#sec:paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:paths">4</a>. Equation <a href="#eq:good-prob" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:good-prob">[eq:good-prob]</a> gives the required joint bound with any sufficiently small fixed $p$, by increasing the threshold for $D$. All possible mean-sites of tags whose midpoint lies in $v$ belong to a fixed enlargement $Q_v$ of that cell, of side $O_x(R)$; this follows from the individual tubes. The same holds for $w'$. Since $K/R\to\infty$ in the thermodynamic limit, the displacements from any site of $Q_v$ to any site of $Q_{w'}$ belong to the wider interval in Proposition <a href="#prop:route-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:route-input">18</a>.

Choose $r_c$ large enough, in terms of $b_0$, to contain every possible cross-route interaction between paths in their individual tubes, as well as the bounded offsets between sites and means. Take $q=2$ to allow the adjacent labels used by a paired insertion. These choices are fixed once $x$ is fixed. We retain a bad-free margin of radius $C_xR$ around both cells and impose the route-availability events for every endpoint site in their enlarged cubes, for both orientations. There are only polynomially many such sites in $R$. Thus <a href="#eq:route-rejection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:route-rejection">[eq:route-rejection]</a> bounds the additional rejection by $o_K(1)+C_xR^{C_x}e^{-c_xR}$. This extra retention can only decrease the nonnegative quantities in <a href="#eq:route-exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:route-exponential">[eq:route-exponential]</a>–<a href="#eq:route-rare" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:route-rare">[eq:route-rare]</a>.

We always sample the routes by the construction of Proposition <a href="#prop:route-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:route-input">18</a>, conditional on the datum and the endpoint sites. In a likelihood product, the datum is shared and the route randomizations are independent. In particular, we do not condition their law on a subsequent successful path insertion. The exponent $c_g$ is independent of $x$, although the constants and $\kappa$ may depend on $x$.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### A common measure with retained end portions

Use the tag weight <a href="#eq:tag-pair-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tag-pair-weight">[eq:tag-pair-weight]</a> for $(v,w')\in\mathcal P_R$, retained data, and distinct tags $i_0,i_m\in I$. On the sampled route from their mean-sites choose one interior label at each intermediate site, uniformly and independently given the tags and sites, excluding the two tags. The resulting list $i_0,\ldots,i_m$ has distinct labels; each interior choice has atom at most $C_x/(Df_0)$. Successive means are at bounded absolute distance.

Write the initial silent portion of the route as $k<a_0$, and its terminal silent portion as $k>a_1$. Thus $a_0$ is one plus the last visit within distance $H$ of the initial site, while $a_1$ is one less than the first index in the terminal portion. Both tags lie in these portions, and the portions are disjoint. Keep all unlisted paths in both copies. Keep the prefix originals $k<a_0$ on the left, and the suffix originals $k>a_1$ on the right. The remaining paths are generated as follows, pairing left label $i_k$ with right label $i_{k-1}$ for $1\le k\le m$.

1.  For $a_0\le k\le a_1+1$, propose a common uniform midpoint in a unit cell within bounded distance of both means and propose the two paths independently conditional on that midpoint. Condition jointly on their individual cutoffs, the checks <a href="#eq:soft-check" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:soft-check">[eq:soft-check]</a> against retained and previously generated paths in their respective copies, and midpoint separation by $s_1$ from those opponents. These middle pairs are probability kernels: Proposition <a href="#prop:insertion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:insertion">12</a>, Assertion 2, bounds each success probability below by $1/2$. Use any fixed order for them.

2.  In every other pair, one path is a retained original. Pin the other path to its midpoint $y$. Give this step mass zero unless $y$ is within $8b_0$ of the new label’s mean and is $s_1$-separated from all opponent midpoints already present in the copy receiving the new path. If these conditions hold, propose the bridge pinned at $y$, impose its individual cutoffs and checks <a href="#eq:soft-check" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:soft-check">[eq:soft-check]</a>, and divide by <a id="eq:pin-denominator"></a>$$\label{eq:pin-denominator}
     \max\{1/2,p_{\rm pin}\},$$ where $p_{\rm pin}$ is their success probability under the pinned proposal. This is a subprobability kernel. Perform these steps after all middle pairs, in fixed orders. Prefix pins are inserted on the right and suffix pins on the left; neither family uses paths generated by the other family.

The final copies satisfy all conditional reference checks. Every left midpoint except that of $i_0$ agrees with its paired right midpoint, and every right midpoint except that of $i_m$ is paired. Deleting these respective tags and using the common ordering of the bath coordinates therefore gives one measure on the bath with lifts to both augmented references. Figure <a href="#fig:silent-transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:silent-transport">2</a> shows the pairing and the retained end portions.

<figure id="fig:silent-transport">

<figcaption>A schematic long comparison with $a_{0} = 2$ and $a_{1} = 4$. The left prefix and right suffix retain their original paths. Central pairs receive common proposed midpoints; an end pin copies the midpoint of its retained partner in the other copy. The arrows indicate the source of a pin, not equality of full paths. The changed left set is ${i_{2}, …, i_{6}}$, which excludes the common first tag. Deleting the crossed tags again leaves identical midpoint multisets.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-silent-transport"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:silent-transport`)

 particle/.style={circle,draw,minimum size=7mm,inner sep=1pt,font=\small},
 made/.style={particle,fill=blue!12,draw=blue!65!black},
 pinned/.style={particle,fill=orange!18,draw=orange!70!black},
 old/.style={particle,fill=gray!15,draw=gray!65!black}]
\node[anchor=east] at (-.6,1) {left};
\node[anchor=east] at (-.6,0) {right};
\foreach \k in {0,1}{\node[old] (L\k) at (\k,1) {$i_\k$};}
\foreach \k in {2,3,4,5}{\node[made] (L\k) at (\k,1) {$i_\k$};}
\node[pinned] (L6) at (6,1) {$i_6$};
\node[pinned] (R0) at (0,0) {$i_0$};
\foreach \k in {1,2,3,4}{\node[made] (R\k) at (\k,0) {$i_\k$};}
\foreach \k in {5,6}{\node[old] (R\k) at (\k,0) {$i_\k$};}
\foreach \k/\j in {2/1,3/2,4/3,5/4}{\draw[blue!65!black,thick] (L\k)--(R\j);}
\draw[orange!70!black,thick,->] (L1)--(R0);
\draw[orange!70!black,thick,->] (R5)--(L6);

\draw[decorate,decoration={brace,amplitude=4pt},gray!65!black]
 (-.35,1.45)--(1.35,1.45) node[midway,above=5pt,font=\small] {silent prefix};
\draw[decorate,decoration={brace,amplitude=4pt},gray!65!black]
 (4.65,1.45)--(6.35,1.45) node[midway,above=5pt,font=\small] {silent suffix};

 {gray: retained originals\qquad blue: jointly generated pairs\\
 orange: paths pinned to a retained midpoint};

Visual witness description of PDF page 39 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A diagram showing two horizontal rows of nodes labeled "left" (top) and "right" (bottom), each containing 7 circular nodes indexed $i_0, i_1, i_2, i_3, i_4, i_5, i_6$.
Above the left row, horizontal curly braces indicate "silent prefix" spanning $i_0, i_1$ and "silent suffix" spanning $i_5, i_6$.
In the left row: $i_0$ is gray with a diagonal slash; $i_1$ is gray; $i_2, i_3, i_4, i_5$ are blue; $i_6$ is orange.
In the right row: $i_0$ is orange; $i_1, i_2, i_3, i_4$ are blue; $i_5$ is gray; $i_6$ is gray with a diagonal slash.
Diagonal lines connect the four central pairs: right $i_1$ to left $i_2$, right $i_2$ to left $i_3$, right $i_3$ to left $i_4$, and right $i_4$ to left $i_5$.
Arrows show pin assignments: from left $i_1$ down-left to right $i_0$, and from right $i_5$ up-right to left $i_6$.
A legend below the diagram reads: "gray: retained originals", "blue: jointly generated pairs", and "orange: paths pinned to a retained midpoint".
```


The silent portions have two purposes. They keep the common tag outside the changed set in a likelihood product, and they ensure that the changed sets start far enough from their common end for <a href="#eq:route-rare" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:route-rare">[eq:route-rare]</a> to apply. The opposite-copy pins preserve the matching of bath midpoints across these retained portions.

<a id="stmt-lem-long-mass"></a>
<div id="lem:long-mass" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:long-mass"></a>
**Lemma 19**. *The common measure just constructed has mass satisfying $$\label{eq:long-mass}
 \operatorname{mass}\ge \mathbb E[A_vA_{w'}]-CD^{-1-c'}-o_K(1)$$ for some $c'>0$ and sufficiently small fixed $w,x$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-long-mass"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-long-mass</summary>

*Proof.* The tag omissions, the bad-free margins, and the route rejections have the stated cost by <a href="#eq:good-prob" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:good-prob">[eq:good-prob]</a>, <a href="#eq:tag-omission" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tag-omission">[eq:tag-omission]</a>, and <a href="#eq:route-rejection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:route-rejection">[eq:route-rejection]</a>; the margins contain $O_x(R^3)$ sites, and extra relative count factors cost only fixed powers $D^x$, apart from negligible count tails. Impose also the full-path bounds of Section <a href="#sec:paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:paths">4</a> on the original sample in the endpoint neighborhoods of radius $O_x(R)$. Their failure has smaller cost, by the last line of <a href="#eq:good-prob" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:good-prob">[eq:good-prob]</a>. This event is used only to estimate the mass; we do not condition the reference bridges on it.

On this original-sample event, all pins lie in range: the retained original has displacement at most $b_0$ from its endpoints, and successive means are at bounded distance. Whenever the required midpoint separations hold, every $p_{\rm pin}\ge3/4$ by Proposition <a href="#prop:insertion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:insertion">12</a>, Assertion 3. Indeed its opponents are the original collection, with some originals deleted and some generated paths added. Route simplicity and the individual tubes bound the number of added paths relevant to any fixed neighborhood independently of the route length. Consequently all such pin kernels have mass one.

Separation from a middle path was already imposed when that middle pair was generated: in the other copy its common midpoint was tested against the retained original which supplies the pin. The two silent ends are far apart. Every remaining separation failure therefore entails two original midpoints at distance at most $s_1$ in an endpoint neighborhood. Equation <a href="#eq:pair-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-bound">[eq:pair-bound]</a> bounds their expected ordered count in each fixed site neighborhood by $C_xD^2s_1^3$.

The choice of an interior label has atom at most $C_x/(Df_0)$ even after fixing the data and tags. A route visits a site at most once, so we can sum this bound over all endpoint neighborhoods, of total volume $O_x(R^3)$, without conditioning any count on route availability. A tag appearing in such a test supplies at least the same weight through its divisor. The other tag factors are bounded by relative counts. More explicitly, restrict all unit counts in these neighborhoods to $CD$; by <a href="#eq:count-tails" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:count-tails">[eq:count-tails]</a>–<a href="#eq:count-mgf" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:count-mgf">[eq:count-mgf]</a>, the discarded parts contribute less than every inverse power of $D$, even after fixed count products and polynomially many site sums. The remaining loss is at most $$C_xR^3D^{Cx}\frac{D^2s_1^3}{Df_0}.$$ Since $D^2s_1^3=D^{-2/5}$ and $R\asymp D^w$, this is $O(D^{-1-c'})$ for sufficiently small $w,x$. Middle steps never lose mass. Summing the tag weights proves <a href="#eq:long-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:long-mass">[eq:long-mass]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### Conditional likelihoods and removal of cross checks

<a id="eq:long-kernel"></a>
We next bound the second moment of a lift. Unlike a uniform bound on its density, this estimate will have no factor depending on route length. Treat the left copy as the observed, or primary, copy. Its changed labels form $$S=\{i_k:k\ge a_0\}.$$ Fix the datum, tags, route and list, and condition on the paths $o$ outside $S$. With $B_S$ the product plain bridge law and $c_S$ the conditional reference indicator, write $$\label{eq:long-kernel}
 M_S(o;\,\mathrm dz)=\int c_S(y;o)h_{w'i_m}(y)
                    K_S(y,o;\,\mathrm dz)\,B_S(\,\mathrm dy),\qquad
 Z_S(o)=\int c_S(y;o)\,B_S(\,\mathrm dy).$$ Here $y$ denotes latent original paths, $z$ the primary outputs, and $K_S$ the subprobability kernel of all generation steps. The likelihood, before the divisor $T_{w'}$, is $L_S=\,\mathrm dM_S/\,\mathrm dB_S$ on admissible reference outputs: the input and output conditional normalizer $Z_S$ cancels exactly. Absolute continuity is verified below, including for the pins.

For an upper bound we may integrate out the pins generated on the right at the silent prefix and replace their total mass by one. No primary middle or pin step uses those paths. We use the same notation $M_S$ for this enlarged measure when taking upper bounds.

Take a second, independently sampled route and list with the same first tag $i_0=i$. Denote its changed set by $T$ and the two terminal tags by $j_1,j_2$. Condition now on all paths outside $U=S\cup T$, as well as the datum. In the $S$ construction remove all checks against outside labels in $T\setminus S$, both from the latent reference and from every proposal and success normalization. In the $T$ construction remove checks against $S\setminus T$. Write $A_S^-,Z_S^-,M_S^-$ for the resulting reference indicator, normalizer, and unnormalized output measure, and similarly for $T$. These two constructions now use only their own latent and generated variables and the fixed paths outside $U$.

This independence also holds at the edge of a silent prefix. Its right pins have been integrated out. If an old prefix path is removed as an outside obstacle, no other part of the construction uses that trajectory. A right middle partner uses the fixed endpoints of its label, not its old trajectory. Routes and label choices use only data, sites, and tag identities, all fixed here.

Let $Z_U=\int c_U\,\,\mathrm dB_U$ be the union reference normalizer. List all checks affected by the removals: reference checks and checks of latent or generated paths against removed old obstacles, including those in the success probabilities. Every such check requires two route vertices within $r_c$, allowing the fixed step offsets in $J_*$. There are at most $C_xJ_*$ such checks, with bounded multiplicity. The simplicity of each route gives this bound regardless of its length. Only distinct-label checks are listed; in particular, a proposal never gains a check against its own old trajectory if that trajectory was not an obstacle originally.

<a id="eq:cross-check-cost"></a>
Let $B_{ST}$ be the sum, with these bounded multiplicities, of the corresponding costs $\beta_{lk}$ from <a href="#eq:beta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:beta">[eq:beta]</a>. For pin checks also sum their costs $m(y,\omega)$ from <a href="#eq:midpoint-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:midpoint-cost">[eq:midpoint-cost]</a>, writing the result as $M_{ST}'$. The first quantity depends on the fixed endpoints and labels; the second can depend on latent or generated midpoints. Uniformly, $$\label{eq:cross-check-cost}
 B_{ST}+M_{ST}'\le C_xD^{-.19}J_c.$$

The following estimate is the aim of the conditional comparison. For disjoint changed sets it preserves the original tag product, with an error charged only to cross-route checks. For overlapping sets it charges a density bound only to the shared output labels. Put $G_D=C_xD^{C\sqrt x}$, with the constants sufficiently large.

<a id="stmt-lem-long-product"></a>
<div id="lem:long-product" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:long-product"></a>
<a id="eq:long-product-overlap"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Conditional product bound). *Conditional on $\mathcal D$ and the paths outside $U=S\cup T$, the two likelihoods satisfy $$\begin{aligned}
 S\cap T=\varnothing:\quad
 \mathbb E[L_SL_T\mid U^c]
 &\le \mathbb E[h_{w'j_1}h_{w'j_2}\mid U^c]
   +Ce^{C_xD^{-.19}J_c}
       \bigl(B_{ST}+C_xJ_*b_D(1+t^{-3/2})\bigr),
       \label{eq:long-product}\\
 |S\cap T|=q\ge1:\quad
 \mathbb E[L_SL_T\mid U^c]
 &\le e^{C_xD^{-.19}J_c}G_D^q.
       \label{eq:long-product-overlap}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

We prove the lemma through three comparisons: first of the reference normalizers, then of the generation kernels when cross checks are removed, and finally of the primary output densities on shared labels.

<a id="stmt-lem-normalizer-comparison"></a>
<div id="lem:normalizer-comparison" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:long-normalizer"></a>
<a id="eq:reference-difference"></a>
**Lemma 21**. *For these conditional reference laws, $$\label{eq:long-normalizer}
 \frac{Z_S^-Z_T^-}{Z_U}
       \le C^{|S\cap T|}e^{CB_{ST}}.$$ If $S\cap T=\varnothing$, then for any measurable $0\le F\le1$ of the latent originals, $$\label{eq:reference-difference}
 0\le \frac1{Z_U}\int F(A_S^-A_T^--c_U)\,\,\mathrm dB_U
          \le CB_{ST}e^{CB_{ST}}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-normalizer-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-normalizer-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* For disjoint sets, $Z_S^-Z_T^-$ is the reference integral with the cross checks removed. Add these checks one at a time. Conditional single-label insertion gives density domination by a constant times the corresponding plain bridge, uniformly with any subset of checks retained. A newly added check therefore fails with probability at most $C\beta_{lk}$. Since the maximum of these costs tends to zero, the loss of normalizer is at most $e^{C\beta_{lk}}$ at that step. Multiplication proves <a href="#eq:long-normalizer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:long-normalizer">[eq:long-normalizer]</a> in the disjoint case. Summing the integrals lost when the checks are restored gives <a href="#eq:reference-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:reference-difference">[eq:reference-difference]</a>. All normalizers are positive by successive insertion; no lower bound uniform in the number of labels is being used.

If $q=|S\cap T|>0$, first delete the shared labels from both separate integrals, which increases them because the integrands are indicators. Compare the remaining disjoint integrals as above. Insert the $q$ shared labels into the union reference, one at a time; each insertion costs at most a fixed inverse success probability. Their total expense is $C^q$, proving <a href="#eq:long-normalizer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:long-normalizer">[eq:long-normalizer]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:protocol-comparison"></a>
There is also a comparison before any generated variables are integrated. The original two constructions are dominated by the separate constructions times a factor bounded in both forms $$\label{eq:protocol-comparison}
 e^{C_xD^{-.19}J_c},\qquad
 1+Ce^{C_xD^{-.19}J_c}(B_{ST}+M_{ST}').$$ To prove this, fix a history at an affected transition. Removing checks increases its success probability from $p$ to $p^-$. For a middle step $p\ge1/2$; for a pin the denominator is the function $\max(1/2,p)$, which is Lipschitz and bounded below by $1/2$. Thus the expense in the likelihood density is at most $$\frac{\max(1/2,p^-)}{\max(1/2,p)}\le1+2(p^--p),$$ with the same estimate for the uncapped middle denominators. Removing the preliminary pin-separation indicator only enlarges the integrand. For a middle step, integrate the removed checks against its common uniform midpoint proposal; Proposition <a href="#prop:insertion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:insertion">12</a> bounds the difference by the sum of $C\beta_{lk}$, including failures of midpoint separation. For a pin step the same bound includes its costs <a href="#eq:midpoint-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:midpoint-cost">[eq:midpoint-cost]</a>; the needed midpoint separation holds on the support of the old integrand. Products of these bounds, and <a href="#eq:cross-check-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cross-check-cost">[eq:cross-check-cost]</a>, prove <a href="#eq:protocol-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:protocol-comparison">[eq:protocol-comparison]</a>. Indicators of fewer reference checks only enlarge an integrand. Every unaffected transition has ratio exactly one.

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### Primary output densities and the conditional product bound

<a id="eq:primary-density"></a>
We need one density estimate when two changed sets share labels. For any $q$ specified primary labels, the marginal of $M_S^-/Z_S^-$ has density at most $$\label{eq:primary-density}
 G_D^q\quad\hbox{against their product plain bridges}.$$ To prove it, omit the terminal tag indicator, so the latent originals have their normalized separate reference law. Fix those originals, denoted by $y$, and let $K_S^-$ denote the separate generation kernel. Divide the specified primary indices into middle indices $A_{\rm mid}$ and pin indices $A_{\rm pin}$. A primary pin with index $k$ uses the midpoint of the retained original label $i_{k-1}$ in the right suffix. Write $\mathcal R_k=\{\xi:|\xi-m_{i_k}|\le8b_0\}$ for its pin range in the compatible lift.

<a id="eq:primary-conditional-majorant"></a>
Conditional on the generation history, a specified middle output is bounded by $C_xD^{Cx}B_{i_k}$, by <a href="#eq:local-proposal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-proposal">[eq:local-proposal]</a>. A specified pin is bounded by $2\mathbf 1_{\mathcal R_k}(y_{i_{k-1}}(0))
B_{i_k}^{y_{i_{k-1}}(0)}$, where the superscript denotes a prescribed midpoint. These majorants no longer depend on earlier generated paths. Integrating the generation steps in reverse order against a nonnegative test of the specified outputs therefore gives the conditional marginal bound $$\label{eq:primary-conditional-majorant}
\begin{split}
 K_S^-(y,o;\,\mathrm dz_A)\le{}&
 (C_xD^{Cx})^{|A_{\rm mid}|}2^{|A_{\rm pin}|}
 \prod_{k\in A_{\rm mid}}B_{i_k}(\,\mathrm dz_k)\\
 &\times\prod_{k\in A_{\rm pin}}
 \mathbf 1_{\mathcal R_k}(y_{i_{k-1}}(0))
 B_{i_k}^{y_{i_{k-1}}(0)}(\,\mathrm dz_k).
\end{split}$$ Here $A=A_{\rm mid}\cup A_{\rm pin}$, and $z_k$ denotes the primary output path of label $i_k$. Every unspecified step contributes at most its mass, which is at most one. Thus the displayed product bound does not require independence of the accepted outputs.

The sources $i_{k-1}$ of the specified pins are distinct latent labels in $S$. Corollary <a href="#cor:bridge-domination" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:bridge-domination">13</a> bounds their joint latent marginal by $C^{|A_{\rm pin}|}$ times their product plain bridges. Keep their confinement indicators when making this comparison, so all source midpoints use the chosen local lifts. Dropping the remaining source cutoffs in those lifts only enlarges the integral. For a source $l=i_{k-1}$, evaluate the resulting midpoint mixture by the measure identity <a id="eq:pin-mixture-density"></a>$$\label{eq:pin-mixture-density}
 \int_{\mathcal R_k}g_l(\xi)B_{i_k}^{\xi}(\,\mathrm dz_k)\,\,\mathrm d\xi
 =\mathbf 1_{\mathcal R_k}(z_k(0))
   \frac{g_l(z_k(0))}{g_{i_k}(z_k(0))}
       B_{i_k}(\,\mathrm dz_k).$$ The two means are at bounded distance, and the indicator restricts the ratio to the primary pin range. Thus <a href="#eq:midpoint-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:midpoint-ratio">[eq:midpoint-ratio]</a> bounds this ratio by $C_xD^{C\sqrt x}$. Applying this to each pin in <a href="#eq:primary-conditional-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:primary-conditional-majorant">[eq:primary-conditional-majorant]</a> proves <a href="#eq:primary-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:primary-density">[eq:primary-density]</a>. The same argument with the original checks in place proves absolute continuity of all primary outputs in <a href="#eq:long-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:long-kernel">[eq:long-kernel]</a>. Only the primary marginal acquires this density; a pair with a common midpoint is still singular relative to two independent full bridges.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-normalizer-comparison-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-normalizer-comparison</summary>

*Proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:long-product" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:long-product">20</a>.* Integrate the enlarged output measures against the common union reference, with divisor $Z_U$, and use <a href="#eq:protocol-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:protocol-comparison">[eq:protocol-comparison]</a>. Dropping the union output admissibility indicator increases the resulting nonnegative integrals.

First suppose $S,T$ are disjoint. The leading $1$ in the second bound of <a href="#eq:protocol-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:protocol-comparison">[eq:protocol-comparison]</a> contributes at most $$\frac1{Z_U}\int h_{w'j_1}h_{w'j_2}A_S^-A_T^-\,\,\mathrm dB_U:$$ the two separate kernels use different outputs and have mass at most one. Equation <a href="#eq:reference-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:reference-difference">[eq:reference-difference]</a> compares this to the leading conditional expectation in <a href="#eq:long-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:long-product">[eq:long-product]</a>. The $B_{ST}$ error is bounded with <a href="#eq:long-normalizer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:long-normalizer">[eq:long-normalizer]</a>.

<a id="eq:pin-cost-average"></a>
For a term of $M_{ST}'$, the pin midpoint belongs to a latent source $l$ in one separate construction and is tested against an output path $z_k$ in the other. After the cross checks have been removed, the two constructions have product law at the fixed outside paths. Write $\lambda_S^-$ for the normalized separate latent reference and $\nu_T^-=M_T^-/Z_T^-$ for the opposing output measure, of mass at most one. The source-side kernels also have mass at most one. Dropping the source-side tag indicator and integrating those kernels bounds the normalized cost by $$\int \nu_T^-(\,\mathrm dz)\int\lambda_S^-(\,\mathrm dy)
   \frac{b_D}{s_1\vee d_K(y_l(0),z_k(0))}
 \le C\sup_{\xi}\int
   \frac{b_Dg_l(\eta)}{s_1\vee d_K(\eta,\xi)}\,\,\mathrm d\eta.$$ Here single-label insertion bounded the source midpoint marginal by $Cg_l$ in its local lift. For every fixed opposing midpoint $\xi$, $$\label{eq:pin-cost-average}
 \int \frac{b_D}{s_1\vee d_K(\eta,\xi)}g_l(\eta)\,\,\mathrm d\eta
          \le Cb_D(1+t^{-3/2}).$$ Indeed $g_l$, or its periodization, is bounded by $Ct^{-3/2}$, the inverse-distance function is integrable on a unit ball in three dimensions, and outside that ball it is at most one. There is no need to bound the density of the opposing midpoint. There are at most $C_xJ_*$ terms. The normalizer ratio in <a href="#eq:long-normalizer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:long-normalizer">[eq:long-normalizer]</a> completes the disjoint estimate.

When $q=|S\cap T|>0$, use the exponential bound in <a href="#eq:protocol-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:protocol-comparison">[eq:protocol-comparison]</a>. After division by $Z_S^-Z_T^-$, the remaining integral is the product of the two normalized separate output densities, identifying the common output coordinates only on $S\cap T$. Integrating the unshared outputs leaves the product of the two marginals on these $q$ labels. Bound one by <a href="#eq:primary-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:primary-density">[eq:primary-density]</a>; the other has mass at most one. Equation <a href="#eq:long-normalizer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:long-normalizer">[eq:long-normalizer]</a> proves <a href="#eq:long-product-overlap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:long-product-overlap">[eq:long-product-overlap]</a>.

For clarity, these comparisons take place first between measures on latent originals and generated proposals, with all success indicators and denominators present. Their domination passes to the primary output densities. In the disjoint estimate we retain the latent pin source until applying <a href="#eq:pin-cost-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pin-cost-average">[eq:pin-cost-average]</a>; disintegration over the absolutely continuous primary outputs justifies this operation. In the overlap estimate the two latent integrations remain separate, and density versions agree almost everywhere under the union product bridge measure by Fubini’s theorem. At no point do we assign a product-bridge density to a midpoint-matched pair. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### Averaging the labels and route encounters

Let $\ell_v$ be the left lift likelihood, with zero value on omitted data and tags. On the retained part it is the sum of $\overline{L_S}/T_{w'}$ over second tags, where the bar averages the route and interior label kernels. Squaring it gives two independent choices with common first tag $i$. The augmented reference supplies its factor $h_{vi}/T_v$ only once; this path is fixed outside $U$. The disjoint leading term in <a href="#eq:long-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:long-product">[eq:long-product]</a>, summed over choices, is bounded by <a id="eq:long-leading-term"></a>$$\label{eq:long-leading-term}
 \mathbb E[A_vA_{w'}^2].$$ Here the indicators are the original reference indicators. Removing good-data and distinctness restrictions only increases their nonnegative sum, and the route and label kernels have mass one.

We detail the remaining sums because an estimate uniform in route length is essential. At every possible tag mean-site, the number of candidate tags is at most $C_xDf_0$, by the good-data flagged counts and the bounded endpoint offsets. Since $T_v=T_{w'}=f_0DR^3$, its normalized counting measure is dominated by a deterministic site weight $C_xR^{-3}$ times a label subprobability measure whose atoms are at most $C_x/(Df_0)$. The same atom bound holds for every interior slot, using its actual conditional probability law. Route sampling depends on the endpoint sites and datum, not on tag identities.

<a id="eq:averaged-disjoint-error"></a>
For a disjoint error, enumerate all slot pairs across the two routes that are within the contact radius, including the fixed step offsets. This overcounts the affected checks by a bounded factor. Every such check has an averageable label, and <a href="#eq:label-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-average">[eq:label-average]</a> gives an average cost at most $C_xb_D$ for its $\beta$. In detail, interior choices are independent across routes given the tags; a label in the opposite slot can be held fixed. When a tag must instead be summed, its point weight gives the same bound. A prefix label used by the pair at the change edge is assigned to its adjacent prefix slot. The common first tag is outside both changed sets. Exclusions such as omitting a label’s own old trajectory remove terms; we may discard disjointness restrictions in these positive, capped-cost sums. Thus the entire disjoint error after label averaging is at most $$\label{eq:averaged-disjoint-error}
 C_xe^{C_xD^{-.19}J_c}J_*b_D(1+t^{-3/2}),$$ with the deterministic site weights still to be summed.

For overlapping sets, identical labels across the two lists form a matching of their slots, since each individual list has distinct labels. The common first tag cannot participate. A match requires a contact, so the number $M_*$ of eligible slot pairs is at most $C_xJ_*$ and at most $C_xJ_c$. Put $p_D=C_x/(Df_0)$. Prescribing $m'$ disjoint matches has total label weight at most $p_D^{m'}$. To see the joint bound, first fix the two terminal tags. For every prescribed edge involving an interior slot, integrate one of its interior choices using the atom bound $p_D$. These choices are independent given the tags, and disjoint matching edges use distinct choices. If a terminal-tag pair is prescribed, sum that match last using the subprobability point bound. This proves the bound also when different edges match each tag to an interior slot of the other list.

<a id="eq:averaged-overlap-error"></a>
Write $q=|S\cap T|$, and let $\mathbb E_{\rm labels}$ denote the sum with the interior probability kernels and terminal-tag subprobability weights, at the fixed sites and data. Expand $G_D^q$ as the product of $1+(G_D-1)\mathbf 1_{\rm match}$ over eligible pairs. To restrict to $q\ge1$, designate one actual match first. The preceding joint bound, with incompatible prescriptions contributing zero, gives $$\mathbb E_{\rm labels}[\mathbf 1_{\{q\ge1\}}G_D^q]
 \le M_*p_DG_D\exp\bigl(M_*p_D(G_D-1)\bigr).$$ Combining this with <a href="#eq:long-product-overlap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:long-product-overlap">[eq:long-product-overlap]</a> bounds the overlap contribution by $$\label{eq:averaged-overlap-error}
 C_x\frac{G_D}{Df_0}J_*
   \exp\left(C_xD^{-.19}J_c+C_x\frac{G_D}{Df_0}J_c\right).$$ Only matched slots incur a cost; unused route vertices incur none.

<a id="eq:route-error"></a>
The sum of <a href="#eq:averaged-disjoint-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:averaged-disjoint-error">[eq:averaged-disjoint-error]</a> and <a href="#eq:averaged-overlap-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:averaged-overlap-error">[eq:averaged-overlap-error]</a> has the form $$\label{eq:route-error}
 C_xD^{-1+\zeta(x)}J_*e^{\delta_DJ_c},\qquad
 \zeta(x)\longrightarrow0\ (x\downarrow0),\quad
 \delta_D\longrightarrow0\ (D\to\infty),$$ where the second limit is for a sufficiently small fixed $x$. The factors $b_D(1+t^{-3/2})$ have only polylogarithmic losses, and $G_D/(Df_0)=C_xD^{-1+x+C\sqrt x}$, so these choices are possible.

We can now use the geometry. For each fixed common site, the deterministic weights of the two noncommon sites are bounded by $C_xR^{-6}$. Enlarge their sum to a fixed cube of side comparable with $R$ and compare with its uniform average. Equations <a href="#eq:route-exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:route-exponential">[eq:route-exponential]</a>–<a href="#eq:route-rare" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:route-rare">[eq:route-rare]</a> and Cauchy–Schwarz give $$\begin{aligned}
 &\mathbb E_{\rm sites}\mathbb E\bigl[
       \mathbf 1_{\rm retained}J_*e^{\delta_DJ_c}\bigr]\\
 &\quad\le
  \Bigl(\mathbb E_{\rm sites}\mathbb E[
       \mathbf 1_{\rm retained}\mathbf 1_{\{J_*>0\}}]\Bigr)^{1/2}
  \Bigl(\mathbb E_{\rm sites}\mathbb E[
       \mathbf 1_{\rm retained}J_c^2e^{2\delta_DJ_c}]\Bigr)^{1/2}
 \le C_xR^{-c_g/2}.
\end{aligned}$$ For the last factor take $D$ large enough that $2\delta_D<\kappa/2$ and absorb $J_c^2$ into $e^{\kappa J_c/2}$. The common-site weights, each bounded by $C_xR^{-3}$ on $O_x(R^3)$ sites, have bounded total mass. All these bounds precede the data average; the site weights are not probabilities conditioned on availability.

<a id="eq:long-second"></a>
Choose $x$ so small that $\zeta(x)<wc_g/4$. Since $R\asymp D^w$, <a href="#eq:route-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:route-error">[eq:route-error]</a> has total expectation $O(D^{-1-c''})$ for some $c''>0$. The permissible bad-set base and $\kappa$ can then be fixed for this $x$, and finally $D$ can be increased. The absolute positive exponent $c_g$ makes this order of choices possible. We have proved $$\label{eq:long-second}
 \int\ell_v^2\,\,\mathrm dP_{{\rm aug},v}
 \le \mathbb E[A_vA_{w'}^2]+O(D^{-1-c''})+o_K(1),$$ in particular after averaging over $\mathcal P_R$.

The right lift of the same common measure satisfies <a href="#eq:long-second" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:long-second">[eq:long-second]</a> with $v,w'$ exchanged. To verify this, make the right copy primary and read the indices in reverse, while preserving the actual generation order. Integrate out the unobserved pins near its common end; none of the remaining kernels uses them. All conditional comparisons above allow that order. For route averaging, use the common-terminal conclusions of Proposition <a href="#prop:route-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:route-input">18</a> for the same route law, rather than a different coupling.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### Completion of the distant comparison

The reference mass is $\mathbb EA_v$ and the lift mass is the common mass. Subtract twice <a href="#eq:long-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:long-mass">[eq:long-mass]</a> from <a href="#eq:long-second" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:long-second">[eq:long-second]</a> and add the reference mass. This gives, after the indicated cell-pair average, <a id="eq:long-likelihood-error"></a>$$\label{eq:long-likelihood-error}
 \int(\ell_v-1)^2\,\,\mathrm dP_{{\rm aug},v}
 \le \mathbb E[A_v(A_{w'}-1)^2]+o(D^{-1})+o_K(1).$$ Up to negligible count-tail terms, $A_v\le CD^x$. Equation <a href="#eq:flag-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flag-moments">[eq:flag-moments]</a>, together with the uniform marginal distribution of each cell in $\mathcal P_R$, therefore bounds the average of the main term by $$C\bigl(D^{-1-w/6+x}+D^{-1+2x}R^{-3}\bigr)=o(D^{-1})$$ for sufficiently small $x$ relative to $w$. The same holds for the right lift.

Let $q$ be the density of the common projected measure on the bath. Jensen’s inequality under projection preserves both squared likelihood bounds. Since $P_v$ has density $s_v^2$, $$\int|s_v-\sqrt q|^2\,\,\mathrm dY'
 =\int s_v^2\left|1-\sqrt{q/s_v^2}\right|^2\,\,\mathrm dY'
 \le\int s_v^2|1-q/s_v^2|^2\,\,\mathrm dY'.$$ Use absolute continuity for the zero-density convention. Applying the same inequality with $w'$ and then the squared triangle inequality proves <a href="#eq:distant-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distant-comparison">[eq:distant-comparison]</a>.

<a id="sec:assembly"></a>
### From local occupations to the constant mode

The energy calculation determines the occupation outside cellwise constants at scale $R$. We now show that the remaining difference between cellwise constants and the global constant is negligible on the depletion scale. The distant comparison controls variation of cell root mean squares; the local fourth-moment comparison lets us replace those root mean squares by the arithmetic means that define orthogonal projections.

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### A variance bound from distant pairs

Let $s_v$ be the nonnegative bath function defined by <a href="#eq:palm-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:palm-density">[eq:palm-density]</a>, for $v\in\mathcal C_R$. We regard these functions as a finite array with values in the Hilbert space $L^2$ of the $N-1$ bath coordinates. Its mean is $\bar s=\mathbb E_{v\in\mathcal C_R}s_v$.

<a id="stmt-lem-distant-gap"></a>
<div id="lem:distant-gap" class="lemma">

**Lemma 22** (Distant-pair spectral gap). *Let $M$ tend to infinity through integers, and let $(f_z)_{z\in(\mathbb Z/M\mathbb Z)^3}$ be an array in a Hilbert space. Average pairs $(z,z+h)$ uniformly over $z$, over all transverse displacements, and over first-coordinate displacements $h_1$ in $[.30M,.45M]$. For all sufficiently large $M$, $$\mathbb E_z\|f_z-\bar f\|^2\le C\mathbb E_{z,h}\|f_{z+h}-f_z\|^2$$ with an absolute constant $C$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-distant-gap"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-distant-gap</summary>

*Proof.* Use the finite Fourier transform of the array and Parseval’s identity. The multiplier of the right side is the displacement average of $|e^{2\pi i k\cdot h/M}-1|^2$. If either transverse component of $k$ is nonzero, the average phase vanishes and this multiplier equals $2$. For $k=(k_1,0,0)$, geometric summation gives $$\left|\mathbb E_{h_1}e^{2\pi i k_1h_1/M}\right|
 \le \frac{C}{\min(k_1,M-k_1)},\qquad 1\le k_1<M.$$ This gives a positive lower bound when the denominator exceeds a fixed constant. For each of the finitely many remaining nonzero frequencies (measured from either end of the frequency interval), the multiplier converges to the average of $|e^{2\pi i kt}-1|^2$ over $t\in[.30,.45]$. That integral is strictly positive. The finite minimum is positive, completing the proof also for Hilbert-valued arrays. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:rms-variance"></a>
Apply the lemma with $M=K/R$ and use <a href="#eq:distant-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distant-comparison">[eq:distant-comparison]</a>. The result is $$\label{eq:rms-variance}
 \mathbb E_{v\in\mathcal C_R}\|s_v-\bar s\|^2=o(D^{-1})+o_K(1).$$ The scale-independent spectral gap here is the reason to average macroscopically separated pairs. Nearest-neighbor squared differences alone would introduce a volume-dependent factor.

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### Arithmetic means and root mean squares

<a id="eq:micro-rms"></a>
Put $F(y,Y')=\sqrt V\,\Phi(y,Y')$, where $Y'$ denotes the bath coordinates. The first-coordinate spatial measure is now normalized as $\,\mathrm dy/V$. For every cell $v$ define $$m_v(Y')=\frac1{|v|}\int_v F(y,Y')\,\mathrm dy,\qquad
 s_v(Y')=\left(\frac1{|v|}\int_v F(y,Y')^2\,\mathrm dy\right)^{1/2}.$$ The second definition agrees with <a href="#eq:palm-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:palm-density">[eq:palm-density]</a>. The quantities $m_v$ are precisely the cellwise values used by $P_l$. Positivity of $\Phi$ gives $0\le m_v\le s_v$ and $(s_v-m_v)^2\le s_v^2-m_v^2$. For microcells, integrating this inequality and using Proposition <a href="#prop:local-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:local-energy">5</a> gives $$\label{eq:micro-rms}
 \mathbb E_{v\in\mathcal C_d}\|s_v-m_v\|^2
 \le\frac1N\langle\mathrm d\Gamma(W_d)\rangle_\Phi
 \le D^{-1}(\epsilon_j+o(1)),\qquad \epsilon_j\longrightarrow0.$$ We must transfer this estimate to an $R$-cell. Averaging the $m_v$ over its microcells gives its arithmetic mean exactly; averaging the $s_v$ does not give its root mean square. The next inequality controls that discrepancy with the fourth-order information supplied by <a href="#eq:local-fourth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-fourth">[eq:local-fourth]</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-rms-comparison"></a>
<div id="lem:rms-comparison" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23** (Root mean square comparison). *For a finite nonnegative array $(y_i)$, with uniform independent indices $i,i'$, one has <a id="eq:rms-fourth-inequality"></a>$$\label{eq:rms-fourth-inequality}
 \left(\sqrt{\mathbb E_i y_i^2}-\mathbb E_i y_i\right)^2
 \le\frac12\mathbb E_{i,i'}
          \frac{|y_i-y_{i'}|^4}{y_i^2+y_{i'}^2},$$ where the quotient is zero when its denominator vanishes. Moreover, <a id="eq:square-root-quotient"></a>$$\label{eq:square-root-quotient}
 |\sqrt y-\sqrt z|^4\le\frac{|y-z|^4}{y^2+z^2}
 \le8|\sqrt y-\sqrt z|^4\qquad(y,z\ge0).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rms-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rms-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* Write $A=\mathbb Ey_i^2$ and $b=\mathbb Ey_i$. If $A=0$, all assertions are immediate. Otherwise Cauchy–Schwarz gives $$\frac12\mathbb E_{i,i'}\frac{|y_i-y_{i'}|^4}{y_i^2+y_{i'}^2}
 \ge\frac{(A-b^2)^2}{A}
 \ge(\sqrt A-b)^2.$$ For the second assertion, factor $y-z=(\sqrt y-\sqrt z)(\sqrt y+\sqrt z)$. The ratio $(\sqrt y+\sqrt z)^4/(y^2+z^2)$ lies between $1$ and $8$; the zero cases follow directly. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:coarse-mean-rms"></a>
Within an $R$-cell $b$, apply this lemma pointwise in $Y'$ to the microcell values $s_v$. Their root mean square is exactly $s_b$. Join any two microcells of $b$ by at most $C_jR$ adjacent steps inside $b$, telescope their square roots, and use $(\sum_{k=1}^m |a_k|)^4\le m^3\sum_{k=1}^m|a_k|^4$. Even bounding each path sum by the sum over every internal edge gives, after averaging $b$ and using <a href="#eq:local-fourth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-fourth">[eq:local-fourth]</a>, <a id="eq:coarse-rms-comparison"></a>$$\label{eq:coarse-rms-comparison}
 \mathbb E_{b\in\mathcal C_R}
 \left\|s_b-\mathbb E_{v\subset b,\ v\in\mathcal C_d}s_v\right\|^2
 \le C_jR^6D^{-1-u/8}=o(D^{-1}).$$ Here $R^3$ comes from the fourth-power path inequality and another $R^3$ from the number of internal edges. The prescribed $w<u/10000$ ensures $6w<u/8$. Jensen’s inequality and <a href="#eq:micro-rms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:micro-rms">[eq:micro-rms]</a> now give $$\label{eq:coarse-mean-rms}
 \mathbb E_{b\in\mathcal C_R}\|s_b-m_b\|^2
 \le D^{-1}(2\epsilon_j+o(1)).$$

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
#### Completion of the depletion asymptotic

<a id="eq:low-mode"></a>
Let $P_0$ be the projection onto the global constant function on the scaled torus, and let a subscript $1$ indicate action in the first particle coordinate. Centering a Hilbert-valued array is an orthogonal projection and hence a contraction. Combining <a href="#eq:rms-variance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rms-variance">[eq:rms-variance]</a> and <a href="#eq:coarse-mean-rms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coarse-mean-rms">[eq:coarse-mean-rms]</a>, we obtain $$\label{eq:low-mode}
 \begin{split}
 D\|(P_R-P_0)_1\Phi\|^2
 &=D\mathbb E_{b\in\mathcal C_R}\|m_b-\bar m\|^2\\
 &\le4\epsilon_j+o(1)+o_K(1).
 \end{split}$$ Multiplication of an inner-limit remainder by $D$ is harmless: at fixed $\rho$, $D$ converges to the fixed number $D_*$. Since $j$ can be chosen arbitrarily large before the dilution threshold is taken, <a href="#eq:low-mode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:low-mode">[eq:low-mode]</a> makes the very-low-frequency contribution negligible.

<a id="eq:scaled-final-depletion"></a>
The projections $W_R$ and $P_R-P_0$ are orthogonal and sum to $1-P_0$. Permutation symmetry and Proposition <a href="#prop:local-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:local-energy">5</a> therefore yield, in the stipulated order of limits, $$\label{eq:scaled-final-depletion}
 \begin{split}
 D\bigl(1-\|(P_0)_1\Phi\|^2\bigr)
 &=\frac1V\langle\mathrm d\Gamma(W_R)\rangle_\Phi
       +D\|(P_R-P_0)_1\Phi\|^2\\
 &=\frac8{3\sqrt\pi}\alpha^{3/2}+o(1).
 \end{split}$$ The equality of the leading term includes both bounds: the second summand is nonnegative, tends to zero, and the first already has the asymptotic displayed in Proposition <a href="#prop:local-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:local-energy">5</a>.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rms-comparison-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rms-comparison</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* The coordinate rescaling, with wave-function factor $\sigma^{3N/2}$, is unitary and maps constant functions to constant functions. It preserves the occupation fraction, so $B_\Gamma=\|(P_0)_1\Phi\|^2$ for the unique ground-state density matrix. In the inner thermodynamic limit, $$D\sqrt{\rho a_v^3}\longrightarrow
 D_*\sqrt{\rho a_v^3}=\alpha_0^{3/2},\qquad
 \alpha\longrightarrow\alpha_0>0.$$ Divide <a href="#eq:scaled-final-depletion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scaled-final-depletion">[eq:scaled-final-depletion]</a> by the first quantity. For a given accuracy, first choose $j$ to make the contribution from $\epsilon_j$ sufficiently small, and then take $D_*$ sufficiently large for all dilute errors and fixed-parameter thresholds. By <a href="#eq:physical-base-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-base-scale">[eq:physical-base-scale]</a>, this amounts to choosing $\rho<\rho_0(v,\epsilon)$. The estimates hold on a tail of every thermodynamic sequence, with no requirement on its rate of convergence. They prove precisely the limsup assertion in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. ◻

</details>
